# Preface.

### Kind Reader!

One should rightly have great hesitation about publishing anything concerning <term>philosophical matters</term><note>In this period, "philosophy" or "philosophical matters" usually referred to "Natural Philosophy," which included the study of alchemy, chemistry, and the secrets of nature.</note>, because the whole world, so to speak, is already filled with them. Yet, because one becomes just as clever from one as from another,

<summary>This page serves as the opening preface to an alchemical or philosophical treatise, featuring a motto defending the "Art" and a brief poem encouraging the reader to seek wisdom through God and concise study. It attributes the core sentiment to the medieval scholar Roger Bacon.</summary>
<keywords>Roger Bacon, alchemy, philosophy, preface, Eye of Providence, wisdom, Art of Alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>Continuity with page 5: The sentence continues from the previous page, which discussed the overwhelming number of philosophical writings and the lack of clarity they provide.</meta>

<header>❁ ) 0 ( ❁</header>

and only the newer authors <note>original Latin: *auctores*</note> seek to obscure the ancients more than to explain them or make them clearer; for this reason, I have not wanted to neglect bringing these present fundamental principles into some order and making them public.

Things that are so scattered and confused make little impression on the senses; however, the more they are concentrated and condensed, the clearer they become, and consequently provide more light and understanding. Therefore, it is appropriate to call these few pages a **Philosophical Theater in a Nutshell** <note>original Latin: *Theatrum Philosophicum in Nuce*. In the 17th and 18th centuries, a "Theater" was a common title for a book that provided a comprehensive overview or "spectacle" of a particular subject.</note>. I do not see why it is necessary to cover up this so noble and highly-praised art so much, and to cloak it with such various seductive long-windedness, or to forbid the naming of the <term>matter</term> <note>The "matter" (materia) refers to the primary substance or base material used by alchemists to begin their chemical transformations.</note> with such very harsh expressions <note>original Latin: *expressionibus*</note>. For by such tactics, diligent lovers of alchemy are only deceived, plunged into useless expenses, deprived of health, honor, and property, and—worst of all—this magnificent and incomparable science is brought into the highest contempt and ridicule

<meta>catchword: ridicule</meta>

<summary>In this preface, the author argues that modern alchemical writers intentionally confuse their readers with wordy, obscure language. He justifies this concise "Theater in a Nutshell" as a way to restore the reputation of alchemy and protect students from financial and physical ruin.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, philosophical theater, clarity vs. obscurity, alchemical matter, education, criticism of authors</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---

<header>❁ ) 0 ( ❁</header>
<sig>A 3</sig>

ridicule, even though such abundant and incredible secrets lie hidden within it that they even exceed human understanding, and certainly the highest level of all sciences can be climbed most quickly and easily through it. For my part, I hold entirely that one can write clearly with a good conscience without devaluing **alchemy**; and so that the possibility of it may be seen, and this art—which has been kept so very secret—may no longer be dismissed as fraudulent, foolish nonsense, I intend to show and prove the most direct path. 

I will do this not only with the **Emerald Tablet** <note>original Latin: *Tabula Smaragdina*; a legendary text foundational to alchemy, traditionally attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</note> and its short and clear explanation, but also with more than 300 selected and proven fundamental rules, where each one explains the next, taken from the best authors <note>original Latin: *auctoribus*</note>. This is so that everyone who reads these with understanding may comprehend all true philosophers <note>original Latin: *philosophos*</note> and their writings or books, and consequently learn to reproduce their secrets <note>original Latin: *arcana*; refers to the hidden, transformative "mysteries" of the alchemical process</note>; in order to thereby [aid] the suffering—

# Philosophical Basic Principles
### or
# <term>Axioms</term><note>original: "Axiomata." In early modern science, axioms were considered self-evident truths that served as the foundation for further reasoning.</note>.

1. That Alchemy is the very oldest science, and had its origin before the creation of the world, will easily be denied by no one. Otherwise, one would have to cast aside all principles, even the creation of the world itself. <note>The author follows the Hermetic tradition which suggests that the laws of alchemy (transformation and purification) are the same laws God used to create the universe from chaos.</note>

2. The art of Alchemy exceeds all sciences in certainty.

3. The grandfather of all <term>chemists</term><note>original: "Chymicorum" (Latin). This term encompasses both practical chemists and spiritual alchemists.</note> is and remains the great <term>Hermes</term><note>Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical founder of alchemy, often identified with both the Greek god Hermes and the Egyptian god Thoth.</note>.

4. Just as all theological books shall and must be directed toward the core of holy divine Scripture: so too must all philosophical books be directed toward the <term>Emerald Tablet</term><note>original: "Tabulam Smaragdinam." A foundational Hermetic text said to contain the secrets of the universe in just a few lines.</note> of Hermes, as the ground and foundation of <term>Hermetic wisdom</term><note>The specific branch of alchemy and philosophy based on the teachings attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</note>. a. **True, without falsehood, certain, and most truly.**

->NB.<- Almighty and eternal **God**, whose beginning has no end, and whose wisdom has been from the beginning and originates from eternity, has, from His inscrutable counsel, created heaven and earth and everything contained therein, visible and invisible, from nothing—that is, from outgoing power or through the single word **Fiat** <note>original Latin: "Let it be done."</note>. **Ps. 33, 6.** In the original text stands the word **Schamajim** <note>original Hebrew: שמים (shamayim)</note>, which according to the opinion of the oldest Rabbis is a compound noun <note>original: "nomen compositum"</note> put together from **Esh** <note>original Hebrew: אש (esh), meaning fire</note> and **Mayim** <note>original Hebrew: מים (mayim), meaning water</note>, meaning **Fire and Water**; and although this appears to be a wonderful and strange mixture, it is nonetheless an eternal truth, so much so that no creature may be kept alive without this **fiery** <term>△-ige</term> **water** <term>▽</term> or **watery** <term>▽-ige</term> **fire** <term>△</term>. <note>The triangles are alchemical symbols: the upward-pointing triangle (△) represents Fire, and the downward-pointing triangle (▽) represents Water. But because fire <term>△</term> cannot exist without air <term>A</term> and water <term>▽</term> cannot exist without earth <term>⊖</term> <note>The symbol 'A' refers to Air (Aer), and the circle with a cross (⊖) is the alchemical symbol for Earth.</note>, but rather where one is, the other must also be; thus it is easy to conclude that when God spoke: "Let it be manifested" <note>original German: "geurständet," an archaic term meaning to arise or take form.</note>, or "Let there be an essence out of nothing or the invisible," the original and mixed **Chaos** of the world immediately came into being. For if one were to say that everything was created from nothing, and yet God was already everything before, it would follow that in

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the line-by-line commentary on the Emerald Tablet of Hermes Trismegistus, moving from the celestial parents (Sun and Moon) to the practical application of the alchemical process. It focuses on the transition of the "spirit" into a "fixed" physical state and the necessity of gentle purification.</meta>

i. This volatile <term>Salt</term> <note>symbol: $\ominus$. While often meaning Salt, in this context it refers to the "Earthly" principle or the crystallized spirit.</note> spirit is the father of all perfection of the whole world. Its power is complete when it is transformed into earth.

> Note: The effect and power of the <term>Stone</term> <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the goal of the Great Work.</note> is whole—that is, perfect and complete—when it has been converted into earth, <term>Salt</term> <note>symbol: $\ominus$</note>, or stone through <term>fermentation</term>, <term>sublimation</term>, <term>coagulation</term>, and <term>fixation</term> <note>These are the four stages of stabilizing a volatile substance into a permanent, "fixed" physical form.</note>.

k. You shall separate the earth from the fire, the subtle from the coarse, quite gently and with great understanding.

> Note: Pleasantly, gradually, very gently, and not with force or haste, but with art and great wisdom, you shall separate the <term>dregs</term> <note>original Latin: "Feces." This refers to the spent or impure material left over during distillation or calcination.</note> and impurities from the substance of the Stone.

l. It ascends from the earth to the heaven, and descends again to the earth, and thus receives the power of the things above and the things below.

> Note: Through ascending and descending—that is, by that <term>continuous circulation</term> <note>original Latin: "circulatione illa continua."</note> or <term>perpetual rotation</term> <note>original Latin: "rotatione perpetua." This describes the process of "Circulatio," where a liquid is evaporated and condensed repeatedly in a closed vessel to refine its spirit.</note>. For what is bodily becomes spiritual in <term>subliming</term> <note>The process of turning a solid directly into vapor.</note>, and what is spiritual becomes bodily in descending.

In the old geocentric model of the universe, this was the outermost sphere that imparted motion to all the stars and planets.</note>, which is called the <term>Spirit of Mercury</term> <note>original: "Spiritus ☿ii."</note>.**

31. Without Art, Nature is too weak to make our Stone.

36. There is a single perfect way that relieves us of all toil and labor.

38. It is one and the same in all seven planets; the poor (namely the five imperfect metals) possess it just as well as the rich (namely the two perfect metals). <note>In alchemy, the seven "planets" refer to the seven metals: Gold (Sun), Silver (Moon), Mercury (Mercury), Copper (Venus), Iron (Mars), Tin (Jupiter), and Lead (Saturn). Gold and silver are "perfect," while the others are "imperfect."</note>

39. Our Art does not consist of many different things.

40. It is only one stone, one medicine, one <term>cooking</term> <note>original: *Kochung*; the process of heating and refining the material, often called digestion or decoction.</note>, in which our entire work consists; we add nothing foreign to it, nor do we take anything away, except that during the preparation we separate the superfluous impurity from it.

41. The most vexing work is the preliminary work:
->To make the materials of the Art suitable for the work,<-
->Requires labor, effort, and diligence, as with other difficult matters.<-

42. The matter is easily found, but the practice and preparation are found with difficulty, as they are not set down sincerely and clearly by any single <term>Philosopher</term> <note>The historical masters of alchemical literature.</note> alone.

43. They say they have revealed everything, except for the preparation; which, as the greatest secret of the Art, no one may reveal except God alone, who gives it to whom He wills.

45. The visit of a friend can be enough to ruin the work, especially if it happens at a time when one must not leave it.

48. ...[me]tal nor yet mineral, but nonetheless imbued with mineral form.

51. All minerals and metals have only a single root; whoever recognizes and observes this does not need to destroy the metals and seek the first essence of their root.

52. The <term>Philosopher’s Stone</term> <note>original: *Stein der Weisen*.</note> arises from a lowly and humble thing, solely from the mineral <term>ore</term> <note>original: *Minera*; the raw, unrefined state of a mineral as it exists in the earth, considered the "mother" of metals.</note> from which all stones and metals grow.

53. The <term>Matter of the Philosopher’s Stone</term> <note>original: *Materia Lapidis Philosophici*.</note> can indeed be prepared from all metals, especially when it is still stuck in its ore. 57. Our <term>Matter</term> <note>original: *Materia*.</note> is a mass that Nature has coagulated and fixed. It has two kinds of impurity: 1) <term>leprosy</term> <note>original: *Aussatz*; an alchemical term for surface impurities or "scabs" that prevent the metal from being pure and shining.</note>, 2) <term>dropsy</term> <note>original: *Wassersucht*; in alchemy, this refers to an excess of "watery" or volatile humidity that must be dried out for the matter to become fixed.</note>. Above all things, it must be <term>dissolved</term> <note>original: *solvirt*.</note> in water and made liquid; it must be moved neither too strongly nor too weakly. <term>Bernhardus</term> <note>Bernard of Trevisan, a famous 15th-century alchemist known for his allegorical descriptions of the chemical process.</note> teaches the entire process most clearly.

58. Why should one then plague oneself for one or two years with the <term>Resolution</term> <note>original: *Resolution*; the process of dissolving or breaking down a substance into its primary components.</note>, when one can accomplish it in four or six weeks?

59. The <term>Matter</term> <note>original: *Materia*.</note> for the beginnings of the <term>Stone</term> <note>The Philosopher's Stone.</note> is the very same from which all metals originate.

60. If you wish to bring a metallic nature to the highest level of its perfection, then work upon that very same nature; there you will find the common beginning of that nature.

61. Whoever does not know the root of minerals does not know the <term>Principles</term> <note>original: *Principia*.</note> of nature.

62. Minerals are of the very same blood, property, and nature as the metals, except that they have not yet been sufficiently ripened for <term>Coagulation</term> <note>original: *Coagulation*; the process by which a liquid or "spirit" becomes a solid, fixed body.</note>, and they may be recognized as unripe metals, because the metallic spirit is found to be just as powerful within them as in the perfect metals.

63. One can have gold and silver in humble things, which are not expensive and are better than the common <term>Gold</term> <note>original: ☉ (Sun/Gold).</note> and <term>Silver</term> <note>original: ☽ (Moon/Silver).</note>; therefore it is rightly said: <term>Gold</term> and <term>Silver</term> do not do it, unless their <term>First Essence</term> <note>original: *primum Ens*; the primordial, pre-material state of a substance.</note> accomplishes it.

64. Our <term>Subject</term> <note>original: *Subjectum*; the raw material chosen for the alchemical work.</note> has all colors within itself, though God has covered it only with a gray smock <note>original: *grauen Kittel*; a common alchemical metaphor for the unattractive, mundane appearance of the raw "First Matter" found in nature.</note>. Who would think that this stone—

<summary>The author argues that the true "Matter" of the Philosopher's Stone is a common, "unripe" mineral found in nature, containing the same spirit as gold and silver but in a more potent, primordial form. He emphasizes that the alchemical process of dissolution can be done quickly if the correct starting material is used.</summary>

105. 137. Whoever obtains the right, true, fiery moisture of our mineral can reach the goal.

138. One must take the <term>Matter</term> <note>original: *Materia*; the base substance used to begin the alchemical work</note> from the mountains and <term>coagulate</term> <note>thicken or solidify</note> it through our secret fire, so that the evil arsenical spirits depart from it and the <term>Spirit of the World</term> <note>original: *Spiritus Mundi*; a universal life-force or "world soul" that alchemists sought to capture in their vessels</note> enters into it.

139. The beginning of our work is: 1. <term>calcine</term> <note>to reduce a substance to a fine powder using heat</note>, 2. <term>sublime</term> <note>to heat a solid until it becomes vapor and then solidifies again, used for purification</note>, 3. <term>reverberate</term> <note>to heat a substance in a furnace where the flame is reflected back onto the material</note>, 4. <term>circulate</term> <note>a process of continuous distillation in a closed vessel where vapor rises and falls back as liquid</note>, 5. <term>putrefy</term> <note>to allow a substance to rot or ferment, breaking it down into its basic components</note>, 6. <term>digest</term> <note>applying gentle, constant heat over a long period</note>, 7. <term>distill</term>, 8. <term>cohobate</term> <note>to repeatedly pour a distilled liquid back onto its solid residue to increase its potency</note>, 9. <term>fix</term> <note>to make a volatile substance stable and resistant to heat</note>, 10. <term>multiply</term> <note>increasing the quantity or the power of the finished alchemical medicine</note>. All these stages are discovered, learned, fathomed, and revealed one after another during the work; from this, it is demonstrable what is fixed or unfixed, what is white, black, red, blue, or green, etc.

140. The whole art from beginning to end consists:
1. in knowledge of the true matter.
2. in the <term>solution</term> <note>the process of dissolving a solid into a liquid state</note>.
3. in weight, to establish the proper proportion for the charging <note>original: *Einsetzung*; refers to the initial "planting" or setting of materials within the vessel</note>.
4. in giving the proper degree of fire.
5. in the <term>augmentation</term> <note>the final stage where the power of the Philosopher's Stone is multiplied</note>.

141. Our matter cannot be treated in large quantities, nor is it necessary.

142. All things have their growth from dryness and moisture.

<summary>This page outlines the ten essential stages of the alchemical process, from calcination to multiplication, and emphasizes the importance of capturing the "Spirit of the World" while removing impurities.</summary>
<keywords>calcination, sublimation, Spiritus Mundi, alchemical stages, coagulation, fiery moisture, solution, augmentation</keywords>

<note>original: Vermehrung; the stage where the power or quantity of the alchemical "Stone" is increased.</note>

298. In every operation, of which there are three, two pieces belong together: 1. The living body, which is the Gold of the Wise and not the common gold, and then the Fire of the Wise. 2. That composite which has just been mentioned, and the secret, hidden, natural fire. 3. The so-called bones with their own water; after the union of these two, the **Bird of Hermes** <term>Bird of Hermes: A symbolic figure in alchemy representing the volatile spirit. When it "arises with many colors," it refers to the *cauda pavonis* or peacock’s tail stage, where the matter in the flask changes through many iridescent hues.</term> of many colors arises, and so on likewise in **fixation** and **multiplication** <term>Fixation and Multiplication: These are the final stages of the Great Work, where the substance is made permanent (fixed) and then its transformative power is amplified (multiplied).</term>.

299. In the beginning we have 3, in the middle 2, and at the end 1 principle.

300. You should know that to this art belong no more than 2 things, of which one is a body and the other is a spirit: or one is fixed and stable, while the other is volatile and unstable.

301. Our stone is but one thing from two things, such as wet and dry. One to soften, the other to harden; one is red, the other white; one male, the other female; one gold, the other silver; one **sulfur**, the other **mercury** <note>In alchemy, Sulfur and Mercury are not the common chemical elements, but "philosophical" principles representing the active/masculine and passive/feminine forces of nature.</note>, one bodily, the other spiritual; one hard, warm, and dry, the other watery, cold, and moist.

302. This divine art has established nothing but pairs. <note>original: "Hæc ars divina non posuit nisi bina."</note>

It details the stages of fixation and multiplication and introduces the "Bird of Hermes" as a symbol of the transitional phase of the work.</summary>

->Purified sulfur and washed mercury.<-
<note>original Latin: "Sulphur purgatum Mercuriumque lavatum." This refers to the two primary principles of alchemy, stripped of their "gross" or impure physical forms to prepare them for the Great Work.</note>

303. The true **tincture** <note>A "tincture" is the finished alchemical medicine or substance capable of transforming metals or healing the body.</note> results in either white or red.

304. The **Physical Tincture** <note>original: "Tinctura Physica"</note> should be composed:

1. of a red, incombustible sulfur.
2. of a clear and purified **mercury** <note>original: "Mercurio"</note>.

**Response:** Take a mineral in which the **Sulfur of Gold** <note>original: "Gold-Schwefel"</note> is frequently found; boil it in a vessel with rain-water—or better yet, with **May-dew** <term>May-dew: Water collected from grass in the early morning of May, believed by alchemists to be impregnated with a "universal spirit" or celestial energy.</term>—for 40 days until the <term>🜍 (Sulfur)</term> of <term>☉ (Gold)</term> has released itself and enters into **putrefaction** <term>Putrefaction: A stage of decomposition or "blackening" (nigredo) where the matter breaks down to be reborn.</term>. Then begin to purify it, reducing it to dust and ashes, and finally re-assembling and boiling it until it turns first black, secondly white, and thirdly red.

**NB.** <note>Latin "Nota Bene": Note well.</note> One does not do well to make the entire **body** <note>original: "Corpus"; the physical matter in the flask.</note> red, just as it would not be good to leave the entire body white. Therefore, when you have made your body white as it should be, take half of it out and preserve it for use; however, leave the other half inside and boil it until it reaches redness, so that you may perform **projection** <term>Projection: The final step of alchemy where the "Stone" or "Tincture" is cast upon a base metal to transform it into silver (if using the white stage) or gold (if using the red).</term> with it.

327. Know that all perfection consists in the **governance of fire** <note>original Latin: "regimine ignis"</note>, and therein lies the whole secret <note>original Latin: "arcanum"</note>. For he who knows the manner of governing the fire is able to complete the mastery. Therefore, learn to govern with a slow fire.

328. In the governance of Fire, almost all have erred—the alchemical cooks <note>A derogatory term for "puffers" or false alchemists who followed literal recipes and excessive heat without understanding the underlying philosophy.</note>.

329. In the whole work, one needs nothing more than the <term>△: The symbol for Fire (Ignis).</term>.

330. Create a vaporous, gentle, steady, digesting fire; not too powerful or surging, but subtle, airy, enclosed, dampened, surrounding, wearing down, penetrating, and not burning.

331. This is the entire manner of the fire, which works "all in all," as **Bernhardus** <note>Referring to Bernard of Treviso (1406–1490), a famous Italian alchemist whose writings emphasized the slow, natural growth of the Stone.</note> has described most clearly in these few lines above all others.

### The ancients have obscured this art with nothing but poetic fables.

1. They have hidden the entire work of this art under the fable of **Pyrrha and Deucalion** <note>original: "Pirrhi und Deucalionis." In Greek myth, these two threw stones behind them to repopulate the earth; alchemists saw this as a metaphor for turning "dead" stones into "living" matter.</note>.
2. The matter and work of this art under the fable of **Demogorgon** <note>A primordial deity associated with the deep mysteries of the earth.</note>.
3. The **Mercury** <term>☿</term> and **Water** <term>▽</term> <note>original symbols for Mercury and Water. In this context, it refers to the "Mercurial Water" or the liquid state of the matter.</note> under **Phaeton’s** chariot <note>Phaeton, son of Helios, lost control of the sun chariot and scorched the earth; alchemists used this to represent the dangers of applying too much heat too quickly.</note>.
4. The two **spirits** <term>Spiritus: the volatile components of a substance</term>, of which one is volatile and the other fixed, they have depicted as two dragons, one of which is winged and the other without wings, yet both unite with one another.
5. The preparation of **sulfur** <term>Schwefel: in alchemy, sulfur represents the active, masculine, or combustible principle of matter</term> they have hidden under **Vulcan** <note>original: "Vulcano"</note>, who because of his hideous form was cast onto the island of **Lemnos**. Also under the fable of **Hercules and Antaeus** <note>original: "Herculis und Anthei." Hercules represents the purifying force overcoming the earthly Antaeus.</note>.
6. Our sulfur, which changes from one color into another, is depicted under the eyes of **Argus**, which were transformed into a **peacock’s tail** <note>original: "Pfauen-Schwanz." The *Cauda Pavonis* (peacock's tail) is a famous alchemical stage where the matter in the flask displays a shimmering array of many colors, signaling that the transformation is nearing completion.</note>.

7. The types of **Sulfur** <term>Schwefel: In alchemy, sulfur represents the active, masculine, and combustible principle of matter.</term>, which in the labyrinth—that is <note>original: "h.e." 18. The small black membranes that appear during the **calcination** <term>Calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to drive off volatile parts or achieve oxidation.</term> of the sulfur are the **black seals** <note>original: "schwarzen Siegel." In the myth of Theseus, this refers to the black sails (*Segel*) he forgot to change, leading his father to believe he was dead. The translator notes the OCR reads "Siegel" (seals), but the context implies sails.</note> with which **Theseus** returned to Athens.

19. The membrane that appears during the **thickening** <term>Verdickung: also called coagulation, where a liquid substance becomes solid or viscous.</term> of the **elixir** is represented by the thick cloud with which **Jupiter** surrounded **Io**.

20. They have hidden **putrefaction** <note>The "death" or decomposition of the matter.</note> and **distillation** under the fable of **Daedalus** and **Icarus** <note>Referring to the upward flight (sublimation/distillation) and the subsequent fall into the sea (condensation/moistening).</note>.

21. The matter when it is calcined to the **highest whiteness** is called the **Swan of the Wise** <note>The "Swan" represents the *albedo* or white stage of the Great Work, signifying purity.</note>.

22. The **stabilization** <term>Beständigmachung: the process of "fixing" a volatile substance so it no longer evaporates when exposed to heat.</term> of the elixir they have hidden under the fable of the **Gorgon**, who turned everyone who looked upon him into stone.

23. They understood the **multiplication** <term>Vermehrung: the final stage where the power and quantity of the alchemical medicine are increased.</term> of the elixir through the **Phoenix**, which is always restored to life. They have said: whenever a **golden branch** was broken off, another of the same kind immediately grew in its place.

24. The sweetness of the **Quintessence** <term>Q. E. / Quinta Essentia: the "fifth essence," the most purified and potent form of a substance.</term> and of **potable gold** <note>original: "trinkbaren Goldes." Gold in a liquid, medicinal form believed to grant long life.</note> they have hidden under the song of **Orpheus**.

<meta>The text ends with a catchword for the following page: "25. Den" (25. The).</meta>

If anyone loves the pure truth and wishes to be the promoter of his own good fortune, let him be highly recommended the poem (*Carmen* <note>original: "Carmen," meaning a song or poem.</note>) which the late Mr. **Johann Arnd** <note>Johann Arndt (1555–1621), a prominent Lutheran theologian who incorporated alchemical and mystical themes into his influential works.</note>, Superintendent at Celle, addressed to Mr. Dr. Morsius in the year 1620, and which he borrowed from the writings of Mr. **Alexander von Suchten** <note>A 16th-century chemist and physician known for his work on the secrets of antimony.</note>. > So that what was first a **stone** might become a **spirit**,
> From whose innermost parts you must also tear the **gold**,
> Which makes the ores pure [when freed] from the **mother's mud**.
> When the **bones** are now separated from the **seeds**,
> Then wash them with **water** of their own kind;

For he speaks thus: Nature is accustomed to always safeguard her noble treasures with something repulsive, page 41 and following. Just as the most pleasant sight of roses is not usually without its pricks or thorns, and bees are accustomed to sting for the reason that where there is sweetness, there is also bitterness <note>original Latin: "Sicuti jucundissimus rosarum aspectus, suis non carere solet aculeis sive spinis & apes ideo pungere consueverunt, quod ubi dulce, ibi & amarum"</note>: If the noblest and healthiest drinks—for example, wine and beer, which are not poisonous in themselves—often give off a foul and stifling smell during their **fermentation** <note>Early scientists believed minerals "ripened" or matured in the earth through a process similar to the fermentation of yeast.</note> or when they fizz; then it is easy to assume that minerals do so even more when they are in their own fermentation.

For such a fermenting **arsenical gas** <note>A reference to "choke damp" or "foul air"—lethal gases like carbon monoxide or arsenic vapors that accumulated in deep mines.</note> not only destroys and stifles the pit-lights in the hands of the **miners** <note>original: "Berg-Leuten"</note>, but often also their light of life, so that they suddenly fall down and pass away; of which such examples are, unfortunately, more than too well known.

This **compound** <term>Compositum|A substance created by the alchemical marriage of different elements, often used here to refer to a medicinal base.</term>, which is entirely and utterly imperfect, is destined for the highest purpose. However, we do not mean only gold and copper here, but also **tin, lead, iron, and silver**. Indeed, as we consider all of these, we must by no means forget **quicksilver** and **antimony** <note>original: *Antimonii*</note>, both of which serve our purpose. These do not merely adorn us like gold worn around the neck or silver girded about the waist; they do more than just please the neck and limbs. They can **renew** <note>original: *renoviren*</note>, nourish, and transform: making a good and fresh body from one that is spoiled, a straight one from one that is crooked, and a living one from one that is dying.

...not only to finish or to sequester <note>original: *sequestriren*; to separate or set apart</note> and perfect the pure from the impure, but also, as it carries the **life-force** <term>Ferch <note>A term used in early mining and alchemy referring to the vital essence, "blood," or spiritual juice of the earth and its metals.</note></term> together with its seeds further along, it is occupied and striving to establish and generate entirely new veins and paths.

How especially this is to be seen in the first part of the *Mining Book* <note>original: *Berg-Buchs*</note> attributed to **Basil Valentine**, a monk of the Benedictine Order <note>original: *Basilio Valentino Monacho ordinis Benedictini*; a legendary figure in alchemy, though his historical existence is debated.</note>, Chapter 14, **regarding the burning of metals** <note>original: *de Metallorum uredine*</note> or concerning **alluvial metals** <note>original: *Seiffen-Metall*; gold or other minerals found in river sediments and deposits</note>. To be sure, the **miners** <note>original: *Berg-Hauer*</note> hew the ore and metal out of the pits; they are raised in such work and always have all the happenings of the mountain before their eyes. ...science so deeply hidden that no one wishes to be found "at home" there. Therefore, I must recount in this place a very fitting **Mountain Philosophy** <note>original: *Berg-Philosophie*; the natural philosophy or theoretical science of mining and mineral formation.</note> from the *Commentary* of the well-experienced **Johannes Walchius of Schorndorf** <note>Johannes Walch (1551–1623) was a German physician and alchemist known for his commentaries on Paracelsian and alchemical texts.</note> regarding the *Little Peasant* <note>original: *den kleinen Bauer*; a reference to *Der Kleine Bauer*, a famous alchemical treatise by Johann Grasshoff.</note>, since such knowledge is likely not well known among the mining folk. From this, they might perceive a little more clearly how the two highest metals, **gold and silver**, actually grow and are generated in the earth, and what might lie behind and be hidden within those aforementioned **poisonous mining vapors** <term>Berg-Dünste <note>In early mining science, these were the gaseous exhalations within the earth thought to be the spiritual precursors or "seeds" of metallic ores.</note></term>, or what they are able to contribute to the creation or **generation** of metals. From this, the gentlemen **Chemists** <note>original: *Chymici*; referring to alchemists and early laboratory practitioners.</note> might take further natural reflection in their own experimental labors, seeing upon what great detours from nature many still wander at this hour.

The aforementioned author philosophizes in his *Commentary* on page 188 in a most natural manner as follows: "Now, the generation of the metals **gold and silver** occurs (for the **Artist** <note>The alchemist who seeks to imitate nature’s processes.</note> must follow this birth alone; let him beware of other imperfect examples) not indeed by the two aforementioned pieces coming together as separate, tangible **bodies** <note>original: *Corpora*</note> and a metal being formed from their mixing; rather, in the **crevices** <note>original: *Klüften*</note> of the mountains there arise..."

...and vapors <note>original: *Vapores*</note> arise elsewhere from a body <note>original: *Corpore*</note> that contains the nature and properties of the two aforementioned components—which are sulfurous, mercurial, and glutinous <note>original: *Glutinosisch*; referring to a sticky or binding quality that holds the elements together</note>—and through the heat of the mountain, these do not merely lie superficially <note>original: *superficialiter*</note> upon the edge of the earth <note>original: *limbo terræ*</note>, but instead rise from below. Awakened and driven upward from both these natures, they are most pleasantly sublimated <term>sublimiret <note>In alchemy and early geology, sublimation is the process where heat turns solid or liquid substances into purified vapors that later re-solidify.</note></term> within the mountain's crevices. Through such sublimation, the subtle is separated from the coarse and sufficiently purified. And as they gradually advance together through those same passages, the dry and moist spirits <note>original: *Spiritus*; here meaning the essential gaseous energies of the minerals</note>—that is, the sulfurous and mercurial—eagerly embrace one another in the pleasant warmth of the mountain, mixing together and preparing themselves for fixity <note>original: *Fixität*; the transition from a gas or liquid into a permanent, solid metal.</note>.

These two metallic vapors and spirits, having been awakened from their certain subject <note>original: *Subjecto*; the source material or "seed" from which the vapors originate</note>, brought forth, sublimated, refined, united, and advancing, find a spacious place in the crevices or cracks of the enclosed mountain where they may rest. ...and vapors <note>original: *Vapores*</note> arise from another source, out of a body <note>original: *Corpore*</note> that contains within itself the nature and properties of the two aforementioned parts—namely, those that are <term>sulfurous <note>In alchemy, sulfur represents the active, combustible, and "masculine" principle of matter.</note></term>, <term>mercurial <note>Mercury represents the fluid, volatile, and "feminine" principle that provides metallic luster and fusibility.</note></term>, and <term>glutinous <note>Meaning "sticky" or cohesive; this refers to the fundamental "glue" or moisture that holds the elemental parts of a metal together.</note></term>. ...of more and continued addition of spirits <term>spirits <note>original: *Spirituum*; in alchemical theory, these are the volatile, vaporous substances that serve as the "soul" of metals before they solidify.</note></term>, all of which are of the same kind and have undergone the same sublimation <term>sublimation <note>The process where a substance is heated into a vapor and then purified as it cools back into a solid; here, nature is said to "distill" the metals within the mountain.</note></term>, since no one disturbs or agitates them within the mountain. For Nature performs her work most gracefully, modestly, and purely, and continues it as long as she is left in peace, or is not otherwise hindered by some accidental occurrence <term>accidental occurrence <note>original: *Accidens*; a philosophical term for an external event or property that interferes with the essential or natural course of a process.</note></term>. This is true whether she is currently conducting her business within the mountain among the metals and minerals <note>original: *Mineren*</note>, or outside and elsewhere among plants <note>original: *Vegetabilibus*</note> or animals <note>original: *Animalibus*</note>.

Nature indeed follows her own path regarding these last two kingdoms and their procreation; however, in the generation <note>The process of formation or "birth" of inanimate matter.</note> of the two perfect metals <note>In the alchemical tradition, the "perfect" metals are gold and silver, which are resistant to rust and fire.</note>, she acts—as has been heard—using sublimated vapors. These vapors rise from the furthest parts of the vein <note>original: *Gange*; a mining term for a mineral lode or ore-bearing crack in the rock.</note>, sweep along, and deposit themselves upon the bed once it has been prepared. In this way, they form a noble hand-stone <term>hand-stone <note>original: *Hand-Stein*; a high-quality mineral specimen or piece of ore small enough to be held and examined by hand.</note></term> or specimen <note>original: *Stuffen*</note> that is entirely fine and native <note>original: *gediegen*; referring to pure metal found in its natural state, such as a nugget of silver or gold.</note>. For these spirits are fine—indeed, exceedingly fine—owing to the sublimation through which they have passed.

one can know whether the much-mentioned spirits <note>original: *Spiritus* — the vaporous, volatile essences believed to condense into solid metal.</note> had at that time completely dispersed, if the source <note>original: *Brunnen* — literally "well," used here to mean the reservoir or fountain of metallic vapors.</note> was altogether exhausted, and if the specimen <note>original: *Stuffen* — a piece of ore-bearing rock or a mineral sample.</note> had already waited many years for this miner; or rather, whether the shaft <note>original: *Camin* — literally "chimney," referring to the ventilation passages or "exhalations" of the mine.</note> still smelled when the hewer set to work with his hammer and chisel <note>original: *Schlägel und Eisen* — the classic tools of the mining trade.</note>, and if this "Duke" <note>original: *Herzog* — a mining term for a "leader" or a particularly rich part of an ore vein that "leads" to more; it could also refer to a presiding spirit of the mine.</note> still possessed a driving force: whether Nature had long since ceased her work here, or was still in the middle of the birth? This is because the beginning and origin of metals, as well as what they consist of, is entirely unknown to them. Even less can they know or grasp how the locks and bolts are internally fastened within them, and with what kind of key they should open them. Therefore, they are all the more likely to stumble and err grossly on their own, or be talked into or deceived by others into one thing or another that runs contrary to all reason or understanding.

Those who work in such mountain mines also experience this to their detriment: how not only in the natural generation of metals <note>original: *generatione naturali metallorum*</note> within the earth are poisonous vapors found, but all thoroughly experienced Chemists <note>original: *Chymici*</note> in their laboratory work <note>original: *Artificial-Arbeit*</note> also desire a very poisonous mountain- and metal-root for their great Universal Work <term>Universal Work <note>original: *Universal-Werk* — the ultimate alchemical goal, usually the production of the Philosopher's Stone which was believed to cure all diseases and transmute metals.</note></term>. <meta>This page continues the discussion from page 100 regarding the deliberate ambiguity of alchemical authors. The previous page ended with a quote from an author lamenting the difficulty of identifying the true "matter" of the Philosopher's Stone, which is described as a poisonous "dragon" or "worm."</meta>

<page-num>101</page-num>
<header> ❁ ) 0 ( ❁ </header>

...and as can be seen among the many cited authors <note>original: *allegatis Auctoribus*</note> in such books, and especially in *The Assembly of the Philosophers* <note>original: *Turba Philosophorum* — one of the earliest and most influential Latin alchemical texts, presented as a debate between ancient philosophers.</note>, the *Great Rosary* <note>original: *Rosario Magno* — likely referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise.</note>, and others. Nearly every one of these authors gives this Subject <note>original: *Subjectum* — the primary substance or "prime matter" required for the alchemical work.</note> a different name. Yet none among them has openly placed its own proper name at the beginning or end where it ought to have been named, such that the Matter <note>original: *Materia*</note> could be correctly identified from it. Instead, they solely describe the same Subject by one quality or another. From this, such terrible errors have flowed, leading one seeker to settle upon one named Subject because it seemed plausible to him, while another falls for something else entirely. 

Since they primarily aimed at describing one quality or power <note>original: *Virtutem*</note> or another, some have described it by its very poisonous origin and root <note>original: *radice*</note>. Others, conversely, have described it using other terms and have remained completely silent about this great poisonous quality, mentioning it with not even a single word, believing that it is precisely through this quality that the substance would be most easily betrayed or recognized.

Just as some, upon noticing this, immediately turned to the most poisonous things, such as orpiment <term>orpiment <note>original: *Operment* — arsenic trisulfide, a bright yellow mineral known since antiquity for its toxicity and its gold-like color.</note></term>, yellow arsenic <note>original: *Auripigment*</note>, vitriol <term>vitriol <note>original: *Atrament* — a term for metallic sulfates often used in making inks and acids.</note></term>, cobalt-based and other mineral arsenics, indeed even to

<meta>catchword: the</meta>

...the most cruel salamanders <note>original: *Molche* — in alchemy, the salamander is a symbol for a substance that can survive and thrive in the fire of the furnace; here, it refers to a specific mineral or spirit found in mines.</note>, because they like to dwell and be found near gold veins <note>original: *Goldgängen*</note>, and [they] are pleased by all kinds of **vain labors** <note>original: *vanos labores* — useless chemical experiments</note>, and [the seekers] carry them out **with the greatest detriment to health** <note>original: *cum summo saluus detrimento*</note>, and yet it is supposed to be a poison, even though it is none of those things.

Since we are writing in German, and because few people have such a book in their hands, and as it is not very long in itself, we wish to include his German rhythms <note>original: *Rythmos* — rhyming verse</note> here **for the sake of better confirmation** <note>original: *melioris confirmationis loco*</note>. This same poet addresses his art-loving reader with this preface <note>original: *Vorrede*</note> for better assurance or certainty:

> The art is righteous, true, and sure
> To him who is God-fearing and industrious,
> And uses himself according to nature's justice,
> It makes him a master, not a servant.
> Do not haste, stay on the right path,
> So shall you have much profit and joy,

> ->For that is certainly God's will.<-

> ->Am driven and refined by fire,<-

> ->Regardless of how poisonous I am.<-

<meta>This page continues the verse-monologue of the alchemical Matter introduced on the previous page. The "poisonous worm" now reveals its transformative potential and its symbolic identity as a dual-natured being.</meta>

<page-num>104</page-num>

I drive away all illness, preserve long life,
Yet against God I cannot strive.
I am the true **Unicorn** <note>In alchemical symbolism, the Unicorn often represents the "Spirit" or the pure, volatile Mercury that can penetrate and purify denser matter.</note> of the Ancients;
Whoever can split me apart,
And glue and seal me back together,
So that the **corpse** <note>original: *Leichnam* — this refers to the "dead" or solid physical body of a metal that has been stripped of its life-force during the alchemical process.</note> is no longer broken,
He will have cause to marvel
At what I can do on my own,
Which for many others would not be possible.
If it should now perhaps be your desire
For me to reveal myself more clearly to you,
I will spare no diligence in that regard.
Only open up your reason and senses,
And do not look at me only superficially;
Not a word too many is written here,
Nor has any been left out in vain.
I tell you this for certain and true,
That never before has it been written so purely and clearly
By many **philosophers** <note>original: *Philosophen* — specifically the "natural philosophers" or alchemists who studied the secrets of matter.</note>.
What drove me to do this
Would be far too long to tell,
So as not to make you anxious:
And first, I will describe my form,
Which follows now shortly hereafter.
By nature I have a **gray body** <note>This likely refers to the raw state of the matter, such as antimony or a crude metallic ore before it is purified.</note>,
And am yet neither man nor woman;
I have both natures within me,
As my flesh and blood show you:
The blood masculine, the flesh feminine,
The power of both together is spiritual.
I have the member of both man and woman,
Therefore I am called **Hermaphrodite** <note>original: *Hermaphrodit* — also known as the *Rebis*, this is a central alchemical symbol representing the union of opposites (Sun/Moon, Sulfur/Mercury) into a single, perfected substance.</note>.

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues the personified monologue of the "First Matter" (Prima Materia) found in the previous page's description of a dual-natured being. The narrator is now identifying its physical location and paradoxical chemical properties.</meta>

<page-num>105</page-num>
<sig>G 5</sig>

My seat I have in the earth,
Where metal, mineral, and the like are found.
Yet I am none of these, as one might think,
But by nature I am thus united,
In the form and shape of my metal.
Warm, moist, dry, and also cold, <note>These are the four Aristotelian qualities that were believed to make up all matter in the universe.</note>
But not solid, rather meltable,
Brittle in fire and in the cold. <note>original: *sprüssig* — meaning brittle or prone to shattering, describing a substance that behaves unexpectedly under temperature changes.</note>
The four elements are enclosed within me,
Surrounded by **Sulphur** and **Mercury**, <note>original: *Sulphur und Mercurio* — In alchemy, these are the two opposing principles (soul/fire and spirit/water) that must be balanced.</note>
Wild, rough, and deadly and poisonous,
Fixed, volatile, stable, and airy.
I am neither herb, animal, nor fat,
**Arsenic**, **Vitriol**, **Alum**, nor **Salt**,
**Sulphur** or any such mineral,
Nor **Gold** or any other metal.
**Philosophers' Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurius Philosophorum* — the secret "First Matter" that alchemists sought to extract from crude ores to begin their Great Work.</note> is my name,
I have only one root and two stems.
Everyone despises me,
Because I have no outward beauty.
In my old gray coat,
I am yet that very beehive,
Which gives the best honey,
For potentates, princes, kings,
Noble, ignoble, poor and rich,
To me, everyone is equal.
And although I am plain and lowly,
There is no other thing in the world,
That contains within itself the true **Tincture** <note>The "Tincture" refers to the transformative essence of the Philosopher's Stone, capable of "dyeing" or changing the nature of base metals into gold.</note>,
For I have **Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurium*</note> clean and pure,
Captured—not mobile, indeed—

<meta>catchword: Even [Gar]</meta>

<summary>In this verse, the "First Matter" identifies itself as the Philosophers' Mercury, a paradoxical substance found in the earth that is despised for its ugly appearance but contains the hidden "honey" of the alchemical goal. It describes itself through opposites—poisonous yet medicinal, fixed yet volatile—stressing that it is the unique source of the transformative Tincture.</summary>

<meta>This page precedes page 105 in the original sequence, continuing the self-revelation of the "First Matter" (Prima Materia). While page 105 describes its physical location in the earth and its chemical properties, this page focuses on its medicinal power and its symbolic identity as the alchemical "Hermaphrodite."</meta>

<page-num>104</page-num>

I dispel all sickness, preserve long life,
Yet against God I cannot strive.
I am the true **Unicorn** of the ancients; <note>In alchemy, the unicorn often represents the "Spirit of Mercury"—a singular, powerful force that is difficult to capture but essential for the Work.</note>
Whoever can split me apart,
And glue and seal me together again,
So that the body is no longer broken, <note>original: *Leichnam* — literally "corpse." In alchemical terms, this refers to the solid "dead" matter left behind after the spirit has been extracted during distillation.</note>
He will have cause to wonder,
At what I can do by myself alone,
Which otherwise would not be possible for many.
If it should now perhaps be your desire
For me to reveal myself better to you,
I will spare no diligence in doing so.
Only open up your reason and senses,
And do not look at me merely on the surface;
Not one word too many is written here,
Nor has any been left out in vain.
I tell you this as certain and true,
That never has it been written so purely and clearly
By many **Philosophers**; <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers to alchemists or "Natural Philosophers" who sought the secrets of nature.</note>
But what has driven me to this,
Would be far too long to tell,
So that I do not make you anxious.
And first, I will show my form,
Which now follows soon hereafter:
By nature, I have a gray body,
And yet I am neither man nor woman;
Both natures I have within me,
As my flesh and blood show to you:
The blood is masculine, the flesh feminine,
The power of both is spiritual.
I have the members of man and woman,
Therefore I am called a **Hermaphrodite**. <note>original: *Hermaphrodit* — Also known as the "Rebis" (the double-thing). This is a central symbol in alchemy representing the perfect union of opposites, such as Sun and Moon or Sulfur and Mercury.</note>

<summary>The narrator, speaking as the personified First Matter, describes itself as a healing substance that embodies the "Unicorn" and the "Hermaphrodite," representing the perfect union of male and female principles.</summary>

My seat I have in the earth,
Where metals, minerals, and the like are found.
Yet I am none of these, as one might think,
But by nature thus united,
In my metal's form and shape.
Warm, moist, dry, and also cold, <note>These are the four primary qualities of Aristotelian physics. Alchemists believed that balancing these qualities was the key to transforming matter.</note>
But not solid, rather fluid,
In fire and in cold, brittle. <note>original: *sprüssig* — likely meaning "brittle" or "splintering" when subjected to temperature extremes, indicating a volatile nature.</note>
The four elements are enclosed within me,
Surrounded by **Sulfur** and **Mercury**, <note>Sulfur and Mercury (along with Salt) were considered by the alchemist Paracelsus to be the *Tria Prima*, or the three prime substances that make up all material things.</note>
Wild, rough, and deadly and poisonous,
Fixed, volatile, stable, and lively.
I am neither herb, animal, nor fat,
Arsenic, vitriol, alum, nor salt,
Sulfur or similar mineral,
Nor gold or other metal,
**Mercury of the Philosophers** is my name, <note>original: *Mercurius Philosophorum* — This is not the common quicksilver found in a thermometer, but the "universal spirit" or "living mercury" that alchemists sought to extract as the base for the Philosopher's Stone.</note>
I have only one root and two stems,
Everyone treats me with contempt,
Because I have no outward beauty.
In my old gray coat,
I am yet that very beehive,
Which gives the best honey,
For potentates, princes, kings,
Noble, ignoble, poor and rich,
Each is equal to me.
And though I am lowly and meager,
There is no other thing in the world,
That holds within itself the true **Tincture**, <note>The "Tincture" refers to the transformative essence or "medicine" of the Philosopher's Stone, capable of "dyeing" or changing the nature of base metals into gold, or healing illnesses.</note>
For I have Mercury clean and pure,
Enclosed, not movable indeed,

<summary>In this rhyming verse, the Mercury of the Philosophers describes its paradoxical nature: it is found in the earth and contains all elements and qualities, yet it is not a common metal or mineral. Despite its humble and "poisonous" appearance, it claims to be the unique source of the transformative Tincture sought by kings and alchemists alike.</summary>

Very volatile, yet white and clear,
Dead on the outside, living on the inside.
A **red king** <note>In alchemy, the "Red King" symbolizes the final, perfected state of the matter—the Philosopher's Stone—or the "Red Tincture" capable of transmuting base metals into gold.</note> makes me stable.
That is the **Gold of the Philosophers**,
In me, you should seek that very thing;
That is called <term>fixed red sulfur</term> <note>original: "Sulphur rubeum fixum." This refers to a non-volatile, active principle that "fixes" other substances so they can withstand the heat of fire without evaporating.</note>,
In short, all other things are entirely useless.
For I am capable of doing it all alone,
And am yet only a single stone:
For I tolerate neither silver nor gold,
Nor am I well-disposed toward <term>Mercury</term> <note>original: "Mercurio." Here referring to common quicksilver, which the "Philosophical Mercury" considers inferior or unnecessary for its own internal completion.</note>,
Neither in the beginning, middle, nor end,
Within myself I complete everything;
I <term>imbibe</term> <note>original: "imbitir," from the Latin *imbibere*, meaning to absorb or soak up a liquid.</note> nothing foreign,
But only <term>dissolve</term> <note>original: "solvir."</note> and <term>coagulate</term> <note>original: "coagulir." The phrase "Solve et Coagula" (Dissolve and Coagulate) is the fundamental maxim of alchemy, describing the process of breaking down a substance to its core and then rebuilding it into a higher form.</note>,
And turn my head toward my tail <note>A reference to the Ouroboros, the ancient symbol of a serpent eating its own tail, representing the cyclical nature of the alchemical work and the unity of the beginning and the end.</note>,
Then you have the whole true Art.
Whoever can dissolve me without distress,
Finds in me both white and red,
Indeed, without any powder or water;
Place me upon a moist bier <note>original: "feuchte Bahr." This suggests a cooling or moistening stage, metaphorically compared to a funeral rite where the "old" matter dies so that it can be reborn.</note>,
Then I will give forth a spring from myself,
Which shines as bright as the moon.
Upon it there grows a **black raven** <note>original: "schwarzer Raab." The Raven represents *Nigredo*, the stage of blackness, decay, and putrefaction that must occur before the matter can be purified and transformed.</note>,
Blessed is the man who possesses it;
Keep the fire steady, and do not hurry at all,
Then you will see many kinds of colors in time.
Afterward, it begins to reveal itself;
When it is at its height, you may praise it,
It turns mercury, copper, tin, and lead
Into good silver that is stable and true.

<summary>The secret substance describes its self-sufficient nature, requiring no external ingredients to achieve the "Great Work." It outlines the alchemical progression from the "black raven" stage to the successful transmutation of base metals into stable silver.</summary>

> One thing in the world stands before your eyes,

> The Man remains fixed, the Woman is that which flees, <note>In alchemical symbolism, the "Man" often represents the "fixed" or stable element (Sulfur), while the "Woman" represents the "volatile" or evaporating element (Mercury).</note>

> It is yet only one, beginning and end,

> Until everything flows and penetrates,
> Like wax, thereafter fine gold is added,
> So the body-spirit is well-leavened, <note>Alchemical "leavening" or fermentation is a stage where the substance is "infected" with the quality of gold so that it can later transform other metals.</note>
> Ready for the work, which shall color,
> The six <term>immature metals</term> <note>original: "unzeitigen Metall." These are the six metals known at the time other than gold (silver, copper, iron, tin, lead, and mercury), which alchemists believed were "unripe" or "sick" versions of gold.</note>,
> And through art, heal all suffering. <note>The Philosopher's Stone was believed to be a "Panacea" capable of curing all human illnesses as well as "curing" base metals into gold.</note>
> This is the mystery of Alchemy,
> Given freely to the wise by God.

...I could tell a story of how such a **Dragon** <note>Continuing the alchemical metaphor for a potent mineral substance.</note>—which was supposedly tame—bit me in the foot, so that I had a great deal of trouble crushing its head and bending back the teeth it had sunk in; nor could I soothe and heal the wounds in any other way than through its child’s blood <note>In alchemy, the "blood" of a substance often refers to its essential tincture or a refined liquid state produced during a reaction.</note> and through newly offered friendship.

For even the **Peasants** <note>The "Peasant" (Bauer) is frequently used in alchemical literature (such as in the works of Johann Isaac Hollandus) as a symbol for the crude, "unrefined" starting material that hides great spiritual or physical wealth.</note> are not so quickly understood as one might think, regarding what they are hiding behind their shields; rest assured, one is always more wicked and cunning than the other, even if they appear simple and plain on the outside.

I also remember quite well here what a highly intelligent and very distinguished **Philosopher** <note>original: "Philosophus"; likely a fellow alchemist or scholar of natural philosophy.</note> kindly said to me regarding the **quality and utility of the philosophical matter** <note>original: "de qualitate & utilitate materiæ philosophicæ"</note>. He wished to summarize the entire content of the *Little Peasant* <note>Likely referring to a specific alchemical text or a character representing a specific chemical process.</note> in a **Summary** <note>original: "Summario"</note> or very brief concept with just a few words and rhymes, explaining it in the simplest and plainest way possible. This was at the time when he was thinking of that same **Writing** <note>original: "Scripti"</note> and how it would be newly published **with a Supplement** <note>original: "cum Supplemento"</note>; it went as follows:

> A peasant is a simple man,
> In whom no pride <note>original: "Hoffart"; meaning arrogance or vanity.</note> you'll ever scan,

For indeed, because of such insolence, one or another distinguished **Philosopher** <note>original: "Philosophus"; a scholar-alchemist.</note> has long since sung his laments: that even after they have planed, polished, and patterned him for a long time, so that they have with great effort and necessity placed a peasant upon the royal throne and chair, he still behaves there

It would be of a similar nature here with the <term>Philosophical Sea</term> <note>original: "Mare [philosophico]"</note> and the <term>Philosophical Salt</term> <note>original: "Sale philosophico"</note>, or the <term>Dry Water of the Wise</term> <note>original: "Aqua sicca sophorum"</note>—that it would be purified through its own earth if it passed through it, and would result in good and quite beneficial health. Whether—as in <term>Monkshood</term> <note>original: "Napello"; a notoriously poisonous plant, also known as aconite or wolfsbane</note> or another poisonous herb—the juice in its root and flower is and remains the same poisonous juice; whichever part one tastes, it would not be without danger and harm. Thus, it might also be the case here with their poisonous <term>Lily-stalk</term> and the lilies themselves, because nature does not tend to abandon its own kind so easily or quickly. Especially since such is the case with the poisonous <term>mine-damp</term> <note>original: "Berg-Schwaden"; toxic or suffocating gases encountered by miners, often associated in alchemy with the volatile spirits of minerals</note> which the old <term>Mountain Dragon</term> <note>original: "Berg-Drache"; an alchemical symbol for the raw, chaotic state of matter or the "mercurial" spirit within the earth</note> exhaled from his poisonous gullet and belly, just like these,

I do believe, however, that it can and must be the case here—as in other certain matters (because, as we shall hear, it has been sufficiently proven through experiment)—that through <term>exclusion</term> <note>In alchemy, "exclusion" refers to the separation of the pure essence from the impure "dross" or waste</note>, provided it occurs <term>in the proper way, that is, according to the laws of art and nature, gently</term> <note>original: "debito modo, id est, juxta artis & naturæ leges suaviter"</note>, that which is harmful might partly remain behind, but for the most part might otherwise evaporate as something all too volatile. Thus, the "excluded fruit" <note>The final product or "offspring" of the chemical process</note> may be without any harm or danger of poison as soon as it is excluded and takes on a physical body.

Of this we can find a comparison not only in the aforementioned best drinks, such as the fermentation of wine or beer or the <term>gas</term> <note>original: "Gas" or "Gasse"; early chemists noted that the "spirit" or carbon dioxide released during fermentation could be lethal if inhaled in a cellar, even though the drink is safe</note> thereof—for although their gas is deadly, the drink itself remains forever and always harmless—but also <term>further through a domestic example</term> <note>original: "ulterius per Exemplum domesticum"</note> proven even more clearly with an egg. For although the egg is in itself good, healthy, and harmless, once it has been brooded in the <term>hatch</term> <note>The incubation process</note> for several days and one opens it unknowingly, one perceives such a smell and stench that it could well be or become a poison to a person if they were to consume or eat it. And yet, in such a seemingly poisonous stench, the healthy <term>chick</term> <note>original: "Küchlein"</note> or little hen must be generated, nourished, and born; and as soon as the same creature emerges from its stinking and, as it were...

<meta>This page continues from page 120, which discussed the "First Matter" and "Mercury of the Philosophers." The preceding sentence likely concluded that these substances must be "taken alchemically" [alchymico genommen] to prevent them from vanishing into air during heating.</meta>

<page-num>121</page-num>
<header>) 0 (</header>

taken [alche]mically. In several manuscript codices, there exists a work titled:
-> The Treatises of Stephen of Alexandria, Universal Philosopher and Teacher of the Great and Sacred Art <-
<note>original: "Στεφάνου Ἀλεξανδρέως οἰκουμενικοῦ Φιλοσόφου καὶ διδασκάλου τῆς μεγάλης καὶ ἱερᾶς τέχνης πράξεις." The "Great and Sacred Art" is a traditional name for alchemy.</note>
The *Praxis* <term>Praxis; from the Greek for "action" or "practice," here referring to the individual lectures or treatises within Stephen’s work</term> of Stephen of Alexandria, universal philosopher and master of the great and sacred art. It is said that this work was written for Heraclius Augustus <note>The Byzantine Emperor Heraclius, who reigned from 610 to 641 AD</note>. In the sixth *Praxis*, certain verses are found, which we present here in an emended form (for they are read corruptly in many places):

> I have nine letters and four syllables; consider me.
> The first three syllables have two letters each,
> The remaining syllable has the rest, and five of the letters are consonants.
> Of the total value of the number, there are twice seven hundreds,
> And three times three tens, and twice three. He who knows who I am
> Will not be uninitiated in the wisdom I offer.
<note>This is a gematria riddle where each Greek letter corresponds to a number. The total sum described is 1496 (1400 + 90 + 6). The description—9 letters, 4 syllables, 5 consonants, 4 vowels, and a 2-2-2-3 letter structure—is a classic alchemical puzzle.</note>

Which I have rendered into Latin verse as follows:

> I am known by nine small letters, myself four-syllabled,
> Each syllable has two, except the last which holds three,
> Four are vowels, while to five there is no proper voice [consonants].
> Twice seven times a hundred makes the whole number...

<sig>H 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: In-</meta>

<summary>This page introduces an alchemical riddle by the 7th-century Byzantine scholar Stephen of Alexandria. The riddle uses gematria (numerical values of letters) to encode a secret name or substance related to the "Sacred Art" of alchemy.</summary>
<keywords>Stephen of Alexandria, Emperor Heraclius, alchemical poetry, gematria, Greek riddle, Sacred Art, Praxis</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the alchemical riddle attributed to Stephen of Alexandria. While page 121 introduced the Greek verses and their general structure, this page (172) provides the mathematical solution and the specific word being hidden through gematria: Arsenicon.</meta>

<page-num>172</page-num>
<header>❁ ) o ( ❁</header>
<meta>catchword: and</meta>

It enters with nine tens, then twice three. If you
Know me, the path to our sacred rites lies open from here.
<note>original Latin: "Ingreditur decadesque novem, tum bis tria. Si me / Noveris, hinc aditus ad sacra nostra patent." This concludes the Latin verse translation of the Greek riddle.</note>

Stephen explains these verses mystically, concealing the literal sense. However, it is established that *Arsenicon* <term>Arsenicon; from the Greek 'arsenikon,' meaning arsenic. In the alchemical tradition, it often refers to the "masculine" principle or a potent mineral agent rather than just the common poison</term> is signified, as is revealed by the following diagram:

| Syllables | | 1 | | 2 | | 3 | | 4 | |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| Letters | | 1 2 | | 3 4 | | 5 6 | | 7 8 9 | |
| Word | | A R | | S E | | N I | | K O N | |
| Vowels | | 1 | | 2 | | 3 | | 4 | |
| Consonants | | | 1 | 2 | | 3 | | 4 | 5 |
| Numbers | | 1000. | 100. | 200. | 5. | 50. | 1. | 20. | 70. | 50. |

<note>The table transliterates the Greek word ΑΡΣΕΝΙΚΟΝ. Note that in Greek gematria, letters also function as numbers. The letters used here are Alpha (1000), Rho (100), Sigma (200), Epsilon (5), Nu (50), Iota (1), Kappa (20), Omicron (70), and Nu (50).</note>

<image-desc>A calculation table showing the numerical values of the letters in the word "ARSENICON" (transliterated from the Greek ΑΡΣΕΝΙΚΟΝ). The numbers 1000, 100, 200, 5, 50, 1, 20, 70, and 50 are listed horizontally and then summed vertically to reach the total of 1496. To the right of the vertical sum, the number 1496 is repeated twice. Below the horizontal line of the sum, the components are broken down into Latin text: Twice seven Hundreds (1400), Three times three Tens (90), and Twice three (6).</image-desc>

Moreover, the letters serve for the numbers used if "a" <note>alpha</note> signifies 1000 and "i" <note>iota</note> signifies unity, as Grammarians sometimes note. In Stephen's Codex, which I have seen, it reads:

> "The hundreds are twice eight [1600],
> And three times three tens [90]; and four [4]."
<note>original Greek: "ἑκατοντάδες εἰσὶ δὶς ὀκτὼ, Καὶ τρεῖς τρὶς δεκάδες' καὶ τέσσαρες." This variant total of 1694 suggests the author was comparing different manuscript versions of the same riddle, which might point to a different word or a scribal error in the Greek tradition.</note>

<page-num>123</page-num>
<header>❁ ) 0 ( ❁</header>

And at the end: ->the benefit from me.<- <note>original Greek: "τῆς παρ᾽ ἐμῦ ὠφελείας" (tēs par’ emou ōpheleias)</note>

But this corresponds less to the numbers. And I would believe that certain things were changed, either by the authors or by those transcribing the text, with the intention of obscuring the true word.

Aimarus Ranconerus <note>A 16th-century French jurist and scholar</note>, President of the Parliament of Paris, as cited in Cardano <note>Girolamo Cardano (1501–1576), a famous Italian polymath; the reference is to Book 10 of "On the Variety of Things" (De Varietate Rerum), Chapter 150</note>, found the same verses in some manuscript or other. They may be read as:

->The hundreds are twice eight<- <note>original Greek: "ἑκατονδαδὲς εἰσὶ δὶς ὀκτὼ" (hekatondades eisi dis oktō)</note>
->And three times three decades with seven.<- <note>original Greek: "Καὶ τρεῖς τρὶς δεκάδες σὺν τ᾽ ἑπτά" (Kai treis tris dekades syn t’ hepta)</note>

This is as if there were two hundreds and eight and nine, that is, 17 decades <note>This refers to Gematria, a system where letters of the alphabet represent numbers. The author is trying to reconcile the total sum of the letters with the cryptic descriptions in the poem</note>. He calculates only the consonants and omits the N once. Therefore, R, Σ, N, and K <note>Rho (100), Sigma (200), Nu (50), and Kappa (20)</note> give 100, 200, 50, and 20, which is 370—namely two hundreds [200] and 17 decades [170].

Furthermore, "with seven" <note>original Greek: "σὺν τ᾽ ἑπτά" (syn t' hepta)</note> is interpreted as being with T [Tau], which equals 300, or 7 decades, which again makes 370.

But the fact that vowels are not counted, that the same number is produced twice, that N is omitted once, and that "twice" is read instead of "two" for the hundreds, are all poorly suited to the text.

**Note:** "Our Arsenic!" <note>original Latin: "Nostrum Arsenicum"</note> cry out those who contend that they alone should be called philosophers.

Lullius <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a Majorcan philosopher and alchemist</note>, in the following little treatise, calls the "philosophical first matter" <term>Materia prima; the primitive, formless base of all matter which alchemists sought to isolate and transform</term> **living arsenic**, because it is, so to speak, alive within the minerals; whereas common arsenic, which has already passed through the fire, is to be considered "dead" by comparison.

Whatever is divided by Nature always demands its own property, but whatever is undivided also seeks its own nature. Therefore, those things that are divisible, or have been divided, cannot create an eternal thing; but those which are indivisible and made eternal can also create their own kind. For all births give birth to their like: just as the eternal God has made all the souls of men eternal, which is the reason that they are formed in His likeness. 

But as far as their outward form is concerned, they have proceeded from natural movements, and these are all perishable. For as soon as they are **dissolved** <term>broken down into components</term> <note>original: solvirt; from the Latin *solvere*, to loosen or dissolve</note> and destroyed, they always return to their **composition** <term>physical temperament</term> <note>original: Complexion; refers to the specific balance of humors or qualities that make up a living thing</note>. This is easily seen in **plants** <note>original: Vegetabilibus</note>, birds, and fish, for they are all destructible and subject to death, in the same way as it is in this art.

But if they say that the **imperfect bodies** <note>original: unvollkommene Leiber; a common alchemical term for base metals like lead, tin, or copper which were seen as "diseased" or incomplete gold</note> are also divided and destroyed and brought back to their nature, and that **Gold** <note>original: Sol; Latin for the Sun, the planetary symbol for gold</note> and **Silver** <note>original: Luna; Latin for the Moon, the planetary symbol for silver</note> are made: then we say that our art does this, and not Nature. For even if the art were to lie eternally within the **imperfect bodies** <note>original: imperfectis corporibus</note>, Nature could create neither **Gold** nor **Silver** there without the assistance of this art, and whoever does not know this is in error. 

But if they say they have visibly **fixed** <term>stabilized</term> <note>original: figiret; the process of making a volatile substance resistant to fire</note> the **elements** in a short time, for there would be **calx** <term>metallic powder</term> <note>original: Kalch; the crumbly residue left after a metal or mineral has been thoroughly heated or calcined</note>, white or red, al[ready]...

This is to be understood thus: the sharp **Body** <term>Corpus</term> <note>The physical substance or metallic base being treated.</note>, which is sharp-cutting, is made gentle through the **Solution**; thus it also becomes subtle and intangible, and is called **volatile** <note>original: *flüchtig*; a substance that easily evaporates or escapes the fire.</note>, and afterwards becomes **fixed** <note>original: *fix*; a stable substance that can withstand the heat of the fire without evaporating.</note>, as is written hereafter:

And then the **Master Craftsman** <note>original: *Werkmeister*; the alchemist performing the work.</note> shall take care that he has a strong glass vessel. For all other vessels are not good, those made of earth or metals: And even if they were useful, they would still be of no use, and unpleasant for the viewing of the **colors and signs** <note>original: *Farben und Zeichen*; the specific sequence of color changes (black, white, yellow, red) that indicate the progress of the Great Work.</note> that appear in the work: and the glass should be shaped at the top according to the movement of the work, after the formation of the heavens <note>This likely refers to a spherical flask or a "circulatory" vessel, designed to mimic the cosmos.</note>, thus it is adorned, and into it pour the noble water that precedes all elements; and do not be frightened if you no longer perceive its power after the **Solution**, and thus it is called a **Work of Women and Children's Play** <note>original: *Werk der Weiber und Spiel der Kinder*; a famous alchemical aphorism meaning that once the materials are properly prepared and sealed, the alchemist only needs to maintain a steady heat, a task considered "easy."</note>, and you should cook the mild [substance] with a mild fire.

For it **dissolves** <note>original: *solviret*</note>, **putrefies** <note>original: *putreficiret*; the stage of decay or blackening.</note>, **sublimes** <note>original: *sublimiret*; the rising of vapors.</note>, **calcines** <note>original: *calceniret*; reducing to a fine powder through heat.</note>, **coagulates** <note>original: *coaguliret*; turning back into a solid.</note>, and **fixes** <note>original: *figiret*</note> itself, without any movement of the hands, and it turns black and white and red by itself. But if you wish to operate **for the Moon** <note>original: *ad Lunam*; the process of creating silver.</note>, then you must not work until the redness,

-ten, and when the true manner comes, then stop, and then test the **Medicine** <note>In alchemy, the "Medicine" is another name for the Philosopher's Stone or the elixir used to transform metals.</note>, whether it allows itself to be easily poured into the hard and the soft; if so, it is then perfect.
